I need to add the full citation: Welleck, S., Kulikov, I., Roller, S., Dinan, E., Cho, K., & Weston, J. (2020). Neural Text Generation with Unlikelihood Training. ICLR 2020.

3. **tail_decay TOST discussion**: Need to add 1-2 sentences in §

4.2 discussing why TOST pass rate (61.5%) is low compared to entropy_max/min but k-means partition assignment remains stable.

So the remaining tasks are: add Welleck et al. (2020) to the bibliography and cite it in §2.3 around lines 26-28 where we discuss declining lexical diversity and neural text degeneration, since that paper directly addresses text degeneration issues that connect to the distributional compression we observe in DDR. I need to make sure the bibliography entry is correct before adding it. I'm trying to figure out the best place to cite the Welleck paper on unlikelihood training. It addresses text degeneration through a training-based approach, which contrasts with our post-hoc detection method. The most natural fit seems to be in the Vocabulary Diversity section where we discuss degeneration phenomena, since the paper specifically tackles repetition and other degeneracies that also appear in recursive generation as distributional compression. That's clean and minimal. Now for the tail decay TOST discussion—I'm looking at the results section where tail decay passes TOST for only 4 out of 8 generators with small effect sizes. The reviewer is asking why the TOST pass rate is so low when k-means partition stability is actually quite stable, and the distinction is that TOST measures whether the shift magnitude is small enough to claim equivalence, whereas k-means partition stability just tracks whether assignments remain consistent across depths.

Tail decay has small absolute drift that's well below the drifting group's effect sizes, but it's still large enough to fail the strict equivalence threshold in some conditions. The partition stays stable because the gap between drifting and non-drifting groups is wide enough that tail decay's marginal drift doesn't push it across the cluster boundary. I should add a sentence or two around line 94 clarifying that TOST and partition stability measure different things—one tests absolute shift magnitude against a fixed bound, the other tests relative group membership. I'm wrestling with the page budget constraint—the paper is already at exactly 8 pages, so I need to be surgical about where I add clarification. The key insight is that tail_decay's marginal drift exceeds the TOST bound for some generators but stays well below the drifting threshold, which explains why partition assignment remains stable despite the low TOST pass rate. I could weave this explanation into the existing L90 sentence or find a more natural spot in the Quantification paragraph without ballooning the text. I'm mapping out the specific edits needed: adding the Welleck citation to the references, inserting it into the related work section, and adding a sentence to results about how TOST's low pass rate aligns with the partition stability findings for tail_decay. I should also verify that FineWeb is already properly cited in the introduction, which it appears to be, so that's covered.     "newString": "where their monotonic decline under top-$p$ sampling \\cite{nucleus_sampling,welleck_unlikelihood} provides a complementary signal to reference-model statistics.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\subsection{AI-Generated Text Detection}\n\\label{sec:related-detection}\n\nThe detection literature spans statistical tests \\cite{gltr,grover,release_strategies}, zero-shot scoring \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,dna_gpt}, surprisal diversity \\cite{diveye}, spectral analysis of relative likelihood \\cite{xu_spectrum_detection}, geometric invariants \\cite{intrinsic_dim_detection}, and learned classifiers \\cite{ghostbuster,feat_aug_transformer,reliable_detection,ld_score}; see \\citet{wu_survey_2025} and \\citet{jawahar_survey} for surveys.\nRAID \\cite{raid} benchmarks binary detection across generators and attacks; we extend the task from binary to ordinal depth estimation.\nSeveral recent efforts move beyond the binary boundary (collaboration roles \\cite{beyond_binary}, joint detection-attribution \\cite{two_birds}, creator-editor modes \\cite{beyond_final_actor}, multi-generator attribution \\cite{semeval2024_task8}) but all taxonomize by \\emph{source} or \\emph{production mode}, not recursive generation \\emph{depth}; repurposing their scores as depth predictors reveals high cross-generator variance (binary F1\\,=\\,0.54--0.71; Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}) \\cite{paraphrase_evades,detection_possibilities}.\nOur contribution is orthogonal: ordinal depth classification \\cite{gutierrez_ordinal_survey,diaz_soft_labels_ordinal,coral_ordinal,kasa_ordinality_nlp} as a task, and the finding that combining drifting and stationary features yields consistent cross-generator performance where individual detection scores show high variance \\cite{uchendu_attribution,munir_looking_glass}.\n\n\\subsection{Model Collapse and Iterative Generation}\n\\label{sec:related-collapse}\n\nSelf-consuming generative models progressively lose distributional coverage \\cite{self_consuming_mad,curse_of_recursion,briesch_self_consuming,seddik_synthetic_data}, though data mixing mitigates collapse \\cite{model_collapse_avoidable,strong_model_collapse}.\nIn particular, the autophagous loop formalized by \\citet{self_consuming_mad} mirrors the recursive generation setup we construct at inference time, and \\citet{dohmatob_tale_of_tails} provide a theoretical account showing that even minimal synthetic-data contamination alters scaling laws---predicting the distributional compression that DDR empirically decomposes into drifting and stationary components.\nThese studies address \\emph{training-loop} degradation where parameter updates erode tail coverage; indeed, \\citet{schaeffer_collapse_position} identify eight distinct definitions of ``collapse,'' most tied to retraining dynamics.\nWe examine \\emph{inference chains} where parameters are fixed and only the input distribution evolves.\nTwo concurrent works are most directly relevant: \\citet{broken_telephone} analyze paraphrasing and translation chains, documenting progressive information loss, while \\citet{markovian_chains} demonstrate attractor dynamics that bound distinguishable depth to approximately three classes; \\citet{attractor_cycles} provide complementary evidence that LLM outputs converge to cyclic attractor states under repeated generation.\nWe study \\emph{non-paraphrastic continuation} chains, where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal, and offer a complementary decomposition: where these works characterize \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs, DDR isolates \\emph{what} converges (NLL location) from what is preserved (NLL shape), enabling ordinal classification without per-generator calibration.\nThe independent convergence of all three approaches on ${\\sim}$3 distinguishable depth classes (via Markovian dynamics \\cite{markovian_chains}, information-theoretic loss \\cite{broken_telephone}, and distributional feature compression) points to a shared resolution ceiling in recursive text generation (Table~\\ref{tab:iterative-comparison} in Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison} provides a structured comparison).\n\n\\subsection{Vocabulary Diversity and Stylometry}\n\\label{sec:related-diversity}\n\nDeclining lexical diversity in LLM text is documented in both training \\cite{curious_decline} and deployment settings \\cite{writing_diversity}; MAUVE \\cite{mauve} provides a divergence-based metric for quantifying this distributional gap.\n\\citeauthor{curious_decline}'s finding that recursive training progressively impoverishes vocabulary aligns with the monotonic decline of TTR and hapax ratio we observe across generation depths; these surface features constitute the drifting component of DDR.\nMore broadly, our surface features are classical stylometric measures \\cite{stamatatos_survey,koppel_authorship}; we repurpose them from generator identity \\cite{uchendu_attribution} to the depth dimension, where their monotonic decline under top-$p$ sampling \\cite{nucleus_sampling} provides a complementary signal to reference-model statistics.\n\n\\subsection{Data Quality and Provenance}\n\\label{sec:related-provenance}\n\nScalable data curation relies on quality signals \\cite{dsir,qurating,ccnet}; generation depth offers a complementary provenance axis.\nProactive watermarking \\cite{watermark_llm,synthid_text,aaronson_watermark} embeds identifiable patterns at generation time but requires generator cooperation and degrades across multi-step chains \\cite{chainwash}; our approach is entirely post-hoc, requiring no modification to the generation process and remaining applicable when watermarking is absent or has washed out.\nOur NLL-based analysis connects to \\citet{min_k_percent} (probability-tail membership inference) but extends from binary membership to full distributional decomposition for ordinal depth.\n\\citet{koike_mia_detection} formalize this connection from the opposite direction, showing that detection scores implicitly perform membership inference, underscoring the shared statistical basis of detection and provenance.\n",

1	\begingroup
2	\setlength{\parskip}{2pt plus 1pt}
3	
4	\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}
5	
6	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.
7	The consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.
8	Shape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.
9	$R^2$ values are computed from ordinary least-squares regression on depth-level means (five points per generator, one per depth 0--4), not per-sample regression; the annotated Spearman $\rho$ provides the complementary per-sample monotonicity measure.
10	
11	\begin{figure*}[t]
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}
14	\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Bold line: pooled mean ($\pm$1.96\,SE shading) across all generators, colored by DDR zone (blue: drifting, wine: intermediate, amber: near-stationary). Thin traces: individual generators. Spearman $\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}
15	\label{fig:ddr-trends}
16	\end{figure*}
17	
18	\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}
19	
20	Figure~\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\,=\,30 neighbors, min\_dist\,=\,0.3).
21	Panel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.
22	Panel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.
23	
24	\endgroup
25	
26	\begin{figure}[H]
27	\centering
28	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}
29	\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\,=\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4): a gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Colored by 3-class label: gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}
30	\label{fig:umap}
31	\end{figure}
32

1	Table~\ref{tab:confusion} reports the aggregated confusion matrix from the original six-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B, Pythia-1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B) under leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) evaluation, collapsed to three ordinal classes: gen-0 (human), gen-1 (first-generation), and gen-2+ (depth $\geq$ 2).

7	\caption{Aggregated 3-class confusion matrix from 6-generator LOGO evaluation ($N=27{,}529$). Each cell shows count and row-normalized percentage. Gen-0 is near-perfectly identified. Gen-1 errors are asymmetric: misclassification as gen-2+ (18.8\%) is 3$\times$ more frequent than as gen-0 (6.2\%). Gen-1 text already carries detectable recursive distortion.}

16	\textbf{True gen-2+} & 464 (3.0\%) & 3{,}646 (23.3\%) & 11{,}529 (73.7\%) \\
17	\bottomrule
18	\end{tabular}}

20	
21	Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-heatmap} provides a heatmap visualization of the confusion matrix.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01T2h1qt1bdSAYDmY3jE7KqC`</summary>

```text
1	To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,
2	we repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest
3	in place of XGBoost (Table~\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).
4	All other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are
5	kept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.
6	
7	\begin{table}[ht]
8	\centering
9	\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}
10	\label{tab:alt-classifier}
11	\small
12	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
13	\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}
14	\toprule
15	Classifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\
16	\midrule
17	XGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\
18	Random Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\
19	Logistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	Both tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\approx 0.79$,
25	and even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for
26	three classes.
27	The per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers
28	(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),
29	confirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,
30	not in classifier capacity.
31	Ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, which confirms that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses to improve over standard multiclass classification.
32	
33	\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}
34	\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}
35	
36	To assess robustness to decoding temperature, we regenerate continuation chains at $t \in \{0.5, 0.9\}$ with fixed nucleus $p{=}0.9$ (matching the default) for four generators spanning three model families: Pythia-2.8B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, and Qwen3-0.6B (Table~\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).
37	
38	\begin{table}[ht]
39	\centering
40	\caption{Depth classification under varying temperature (fixed top-$p$\,=\,0.9). Binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+. LOGO evaluation; 95\% bootstrap CIs.$^{\ast}$$^{\dagger}$}
41	\label{tab:sampling-ablation}
42	\small
43	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
44	\begin{tabular}{llcc c}
45	\toprule
46	Model & Temp & LOGO Binary F1 & 95\% CI & $\rho$(ent.\ mean) \\
47	\midrule
48	Pythia-2.8B$^{\dagger}$ & 0.5  & \textbf{0.814} & [.798, .828] & $-0.58$ \\
49	Pythia-2.8B$^{\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.740 & [.725, .755] & $-0.60$ \\
50	Pythia-2.8B$^{\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.480 & [.465, .498] & $-0.30$ \\
51	\midrule
52	OLMo-2-1B & 0.5  & \textbf{0.783} & [.768, .798] & $-0.72$ \\
53	OLMo-2-1B & 0.7  & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & $-0.60$ \\
54	OLMo-2-1B & 0.9  & 0.453 & [.437, .469] & $-0.26$ \\
55	\midrule
56	Qwen3-1.7B$^{\dagger}$ & 0.5  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\
57	Qwen3-1.7B$^{\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\
58	Qwen3-1.7B$^{\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.566 & --- & $-0.37$ \\
59	\midrule
60	Qwen3-0.6B & 0.5  & 0.711 & [.694, .729] & $-0.67$ \\
61	Qwen3-0.6B & 0.7  & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & $-0.60$ \\
62	Qwen3-0.6B & 0.9  & 0.598 & [.582, .615] & $-0.29$ \\
63	\bottomrule
64	\end{tabular}}
65	\end{table}
66	\vspace{2pt}
67	\noindent\begin{minipage}{\columnwidth}
68	\scriptsize $^{\ast}$OLMo temperature experiments use OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release); the original OLMo-2-1B-hf weights are no longer publicly available.\par\vspace{1pt}
69	$^{\dagger}$Pythia-2.8B and Qwen3-1.7B use 6-generator LOGO (pre-expansion); OLMo-2-1B and Qwen3-0.6B use 8-generator LOGO. Within-model comparisons are unaffected.
70	\end{minipage}
71	
72	\begin{sloppypar}
73	Temperature sensitivity is asymmetric and model-dependent. High-temperature degradation ($t{=}0.9$) is universal: all four generators lose $9.3$--$26.0$\,pp in binary F1, with entropy mean's $|\rho|$ weakening to $0.26$--$0.37$ (vs.\ $0.58$--$0.72$ at $t{=}0.5$). Low-temperature improvement ($t{=}0.5$) is model-dependent: Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B gain $+7.4$ and $+8.8$\,pp respectively, while both Qwen models show negligible change ($0$--$2.0$\,pp). This split tracks model family rather than scale: Qwen3-0.6B (smallest) and Qwen3-1.7B both plateau, while OLMo-2-1B (similar scale to Qwen3-1.7B) benefits substantially. One possible explanation is that Qwen models produce more peaked output distributions at $t{=}0.7$, leaving less room for low-temperature compression to sharpen the depth signal.
74	\end{sloppypar}
75	
76	The method operates reliably within $t \in [0.5, 0.7]$ across all tested models. Degradation onset at $t{=}0.9$ defines the practical upper bound, consistent with the hypothesis that elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that DDR exploits (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
77	
78	\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}
79	
80	Figure~\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.
81	Pythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.
82	The scale effect (larger models $\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.
83

88	\label{fig:per-gen}
89	\end{figure}

1	\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}
2	
3	Table~\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.
4	All results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.
5	
6	\begin{table}[ht]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}
10	\label{tab:instruct}
11	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}
13	\toprule
14	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Binary F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\boldsymbol{\rho}$\textbf{(ent.\ mean)}} & \\
15	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
16	\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \textbf{Rev.} \\
17	\midrule
18	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
19	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
20	Llama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	Instruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\Delta| \leq 3.2$\,pp).
26	The asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.
27	Entropy-mean $\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\ 3 of 7 for Llama.
28	This pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
29	
30	\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}
31	To test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).
32	
33	\begin{table}[ht]
34	\centering
35	\small
36	\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}
37	\label{tab:instruct-retrain}
38	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
39	\toprule
40	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Base$\rightarrow$Inst} & \textbf{Retrained} & \textbf{$\Delta$} \\
41	\midrule
42	OLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\
43	Qwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\
44	Qwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table}
48	
49	Retraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).
50	Three-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\,=\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.
51	
52	\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}
53	$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\,=\,0.372 (vs.\ 0.660 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.
54	The compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).
55	Cross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\,=\,0.490; instruct$\rightarrow$base F1\,=\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.
56	These results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).
57	
58	\begin{figure}[ht]
59	\centering
60	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}
61	\caption{DDR partition under base (left) vs.\ instruction-tuned (right) models. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators. Base models exhibit a clean drifting/non-drifting separation (silhouette\,=\,0.660); instruction tuning compresses all features toward zero, collapsing the partition (silhouette\,=\,0.372). Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate under instruct, while formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}
62	\label{fig:instruct-ddr}
63	\end{figure}
64	
65	\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}
66	One hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.
67	To test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.
68	The hypothesis is partially rejected: Qwen3-1.7B-Instruct shows mixed changes (entropy max $d$: $0.51 \to 0.08$, becoming near-stationary; entropy std $d$: $1.07 \to 1.51$, increasing), while OLMo-Instruct loses nearly all depth signal (entropy mean $d$: $1.33 \to 0.02$; tail decay $d$: $0.60 \to 0.06$).
69	The model-dependent heterogeneity rules out reference mismatch as a universal explanation for instruct DDR degradation, supporting the interpretation that alignment fundamentally reshapes the generation dynamics rather than introducing a correctable measurement artifact.
70	
71	\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}
72	To test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).
73	
74	\begin{table}[ht]
75	\centering
76	\small
77	\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}

96	Note that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.

      "content": "\\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}\n\\label{tab:instruct}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Binary F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$\\textbf{(ent.\\ mean)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \\textbf{Rev.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nInstruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\\Delta| \\leq 3.2$\\,pp).\nThe asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.\nEntropy-mean $\\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\\ 3 of 7 for Llama.\nThis pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}\nTo test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-retrain}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Base$\\rightarrow$Inst} & \\textbf{Retrained} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nRetraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).\nThree-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}\n$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.372 (vs.\\ 0.660 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.\nThe compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).\nCross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\\,=\\,0.490; instruct$\\rightarrow$base F1\\,=\\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.\nThese results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\n\\caption{DDR partition under base (left) vs.\\ instruction-tuned (right) models. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators. Base models exhibit a clean drifting/non-drifting separation (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660); instruction tuning compresses all features toward zero, collapsing the partition (silhouette\\,=\\,0.372). Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate under instruct, while formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}\n\\label{fig:instruct-ddr}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}\nOne hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.\nTo test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.\nThe hypothesis is partially rejected: Qwen3-1.7B-Instruct shows mixed changes (entropy max $d$: $0.51 \\to 0.08$, becoming near-stationary; entropy std $d$: $1.07 \\to 1.51$, increasing), while OLMo-Instruct loses nearly all depth signal (entropy mean $d$: $1.33 \\to 0.02$; tail decay $d$: $0.60 \\to 0.06$).\nThe model-dependent heterogeneity rules out reference mismatch as a universal explanation for instruct DDR degradation, supporting the interpretation that alignment fundamentally reshapes the generation dynamics rather than introducing a correctable measurement artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}\nTo test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-qlora}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\n\\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\\boldsymbol{\\Delta}$ & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\\,$\\pm$\\,0.007 & $+$31.3\\,pp & 4{,}429 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\\,$\\pm$\\,0.008 & $+$17.5\\,pp & 4{,}963 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\\,$\\pm$\\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\\,$\\pm$\\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\\,pp), indicating that depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.\nNote that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.\n",
      "numLines": 98,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 98
    }
  },

1	% section header and label in main.tex

3	\begin{figure}[h!]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_nll_distribution.pdf}
6	\caption{Token-level NLL distribution by generation depth, aggregated across all 8 generators (Pythia-410M reference model). Dashed lines indicate median NLL per depth. The systematic leftward shift demonstrates that deeper-generation text is increasingly predictable.}
7	\label{fig:nll-dist}
8	\end{figure}
9	
10	Figure~\ref{fig:nll-dist} plots the kernel density of per-token NLL across depths~0--4 (8 generators, 25 texts/depth, ${\sim}$236k tokens, Pythia-410M reference).
11	The distributions shift leftward as depth increases: median NLL drops from 1.64~nats (gen-0) to 0.02 (gen-4), confirming that recursively generated text becomes increasingly predictable.
12	This monotonic compression provides a mechanistic explanation for the entropy-based features driving the depth classifier.
13

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\nWe hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of nucleus sampling: top-$p$ truncation preserves the conditional distribution's functional form while recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical, compressing expected NLL monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}); (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure}",
    "newString": "\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 347pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=347pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. Dashed lines in (a): pool average. Heatmap in (b) shows the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure}",
    "originalFile": "To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,\nwe repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest\nin place of XGBoost (Table~\\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).\nAll other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are\nkept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:alt-classifier}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}\n\\toprule\nClassifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\\\\n\\midrule\nXGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\\\\nRandom Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\\\\nLogistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\\approx 0.79$,\nand even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for\nthree classes.\nThe per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers\n(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),\nconfirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.\nOrdinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, which confirms that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses to improve over standard multiclass classification.\n\n\\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}\n\nTo assess robustness to decoding temperature, we regenerate continuation chains at $t \\in \\{0.5, 0.9\\}$ with fixed nucleus $p{=}0.9$ (matching the default) for four generators spanning three model families: Pythia-2.8B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, and Qwen3-0.6B (Table~\\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Depth classification under varying temperature (fixed top-$p$\\,=\\,0.9). Binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+. LOGO evaluation; 95\\% bootstrap CIs.$^{\\ast}$$^{\\dagger}$}\n\\label{tab:sampling-ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc c}\n\\toprule\nModel & Temp & LOGO Binary F1 & 95\\% CI & $\\rho$(ent.\\ mean) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.5  & \\textbf{0.814} & [.798, .828] & $-0.58$ \\\\\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.740 & [.725, .755] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.480 & [.465, .498] & $-0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.5  & \\textbf{0.783} & [.768, .798] & $-0.72$ \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.7  & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.9  & 0.453 & [.437, .469] & $-0.26$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.5  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.566 & --- & $-0.37$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.5  & 0.711 & [.694, .729] & $-0.67$ \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.7  & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.9  & 0.598 & [.582, .615] & $-0.29$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\scriptsize $^{\\ast}$OLMo temperature experiments use OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release); the original OLMo-2-1B-hf weights are no longer publicly available.\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n$^{\\dagger}$Pythia-2.8B and Qwen3-1.7B use 6-generator LOGO (pre-expansion); OLMo-2-1B and Qwen3-0.6B use 8-generator LOGO. Within-model comparisons are unaffected.\n\\end{minipage}\n\n\\begin{sloppypar}\nTemperature sensitivity is asymmetric and model-dependent. High-temperature degradation ($t{=}0.9$) is universal: all four generators lose $9.3$--$26.0$\\,pp in binary F1, with entropy mean's $|\\rho|$ weakening to $0.26$--$0.37$ (vs.\\ $0.58$--$0.72$ at $t{=}0.5$). Low-temperature improvement ($t{=}0.5$) is model-dependent: Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B gain $+7.4$ and $+8.8$\\,pp respectively, while both Qwen models show negligible change ($0$--$2.0$\\,pp). This split tracks model family rather than scale: Qwen3-0.6B (smallest) and Qwen3-1.7B both plateau, while OLMo-2-1B (similar scale to Qwen3-1.7B) benefits substantially. One possible explanation is that Qwen models produce more peaked output distributions at $t{=}0.7$, leaving less room for low-temperature compression to sharpen the depth signal.\n\\end{sloppypar}\n\nThe method operates reliably within $t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$ across all tested models. Degradation onset at $t{=}0.9$ defines the practical upper bound, consistent with the hypothesis that elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that DDR exploits (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.\nPythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.\nThe scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1. Dashed lines: pool average. (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap showing the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure}\n",

Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate; formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}\n\\label{fig:instruct-ddr}\n\\end{figure}",
    "originalFile": "\\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}\n\\label{tab:instruct}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Binary F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$\\textbf{(ent.\\ mean)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \\textbf{Rev.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nInstruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\\Delta| \\leq 3.2$\\,pp).\nThe asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.\nEntropy-mean $\\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\\ 3 of 7 for Llama.\nThis pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}\nTo test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-retrain}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Base$\\rightarrow$Inst} & \\textbf{Retrained} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nRetraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).\nThree-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}\n$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.372 (vs.\\ 0.660 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.\nThe compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).\nCross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\\,=\\,0.490; instruct$\\rightarrow$base F1\\,=\\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.\nThese results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\n\\caption{DDR partition under base (left) vs.\\ instruction-tuned (right) models. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators. Base models exhibit a clean drifting/non-drifting separation (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660); instruction tuning compresses all features toward zero, collapsing the partition (silhouette\\,=\\,0.372). Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate under instruct, while formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}\n\\label{fig:instruct-ddr}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}\nOne hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.\nTo test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.\nThe hypothesis is partially rejected: Qwen3-1.7B-Instruct shows mixed changes (entropy max $d$: $0.51 \\to 0.08$, becoming near-stationary; entropy std $d$: $1.07 \\to 1.51$, increasing), while OLMo-Instruct loses nearly all depth signal (entropy mean $d$: $1.33 \\to 0.02$; tail decay $d$: $0.60 \\to 0.06$).\nThe model-dependent heterogeneity rules out reference mismatch as a universal explanation for instruct DDR degradation, supporting the interpretation that alignment fundamentally reshapes the generation dynamics rather than introducing a correctable measurement artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}\nTo test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-qlora}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\n\\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\\boldsymbol{\\Delta}$ & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\\,$\\pm$\\,0.007 & $+$31.3\\,pp & 4{,}429 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\\,$\\pm$\\,0.008 & $+$17.5\\,pp & 4{,}963 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\\,$\\pm$\\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\\,$\\pm$\\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\\,pp), indicating that depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.\nNote that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.\n",

The systematic leftward shift demonstrates that deeper-generation text is increasingly predictable.}\n\\label{fig:nll-dist}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:nll-dist} plots the kernel density of per-token NLL across depths~0--4 (8 generators, 25 texts/depth, ${\\sim}$236k tokens, Pythia-410M reference).\nThe distributions shift leftward as depth increases: median NLL drops from 1.64~nats (gen-0) to 0.02 (gen-4), confirming that recursively generated text becomes increasingly predictable.\nThis monotonic compression provides a mechanistic explanation for the entropy-based features driving the depth classifier.\n",

_source line 3874 · 2026-05-22T10:07:34.579Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   - User (陈卓凡) is finalizing "Depth Profiling of Recursive LLM Generation Chains" for EMNLP 2026 ARR submission, DDL May 25, 2026 (3 days away).
   - Previously said "不继续审稿了" (stop reviews), but Round 46 and 47 review reports arrived anyway.
   - User's explicit requests: (a) "做最后微调" — do final tweaks based on reviewer feedback, (b) "正文超8页了一点，附录中避免大量留白，尽量向上对齐，figure5,6,7，9拆为两个图竖着排布" — fix main text exceeding 8 pages, reduce appendix whitespace, split Figures 5/6/7/9 into vertical layouts.
   - Decision D043 remains: no new GPU experiments, writing/framing only.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - DDR (Differential Depth Response): location features drift (d=0.76-1.17), shape features near-stationary (d<0.10), entropy_std intermediate (d=0.55)
   - LOGO (Leave-One-Generator-Out): 8 generators, primary evaluation protocol
   - Leak-free binary F1=0.694, 3-class F1=0.762 (8-gen LOGO XGBoost), 0.905 (6-gen LOGO QLoRA)
   - EMNLP 2026 ARR: strict 8-page main text limit
   - Paper compiled with `pdflatex` via ACL 2023 template
   - 37K texts, 8 generators, 5 architectural families (0.6B-8B), Pythia-410M reference model
   - LaTeX trim/clip technique for splitting PDF figures into halves
   - PDF page dimensions: confusion (688x261pt), per_gen (694x333pt), instruct_ddr (410x226pt), umap (712x294pt)

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`docs/paper/references.bib`** — Added Welleck et al. (2020) bib entry for W5 fix
     ```bibtex
     @inproceedings{welleck_unlikelihood,
       title = {{Neural Text Generation with Unlikelihood Training}},
       author = {Welleck, Sean and Kulikov, Ilia and Roller, Stephen and Dinan, Emily and Cho, Kyunghyun and Weston, Jason},
       booktitle = {Proceedings of the International Conference on Learning Representations},
       year = {2020},
     }
     ```
   
   - **`docs/paper/related_work.tex`** — Added welleck_unlikelihood citation in §2.3 L28
     - Changed: `\cite{nucleus_sampling}` → `\cite{nucleus_sampling,welleck_unlikelihood}`
   
   - **`docs/paper/results.tex`** — Added tail_decay TOST explanation in §4.2 Validation paragraph (W3 fix)
     - Changed L94 from ending at `($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).` to:
     ```
     ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$), consistent with its stable partition assignment: the drift, while exceeding the TOST equivalence bound for some generators, remains an order of magnitude below the drifting group ($d \geq 0.76$).
     ```
   
   - **`docs/paper/discussion.tex`** — Added `\looseness=-1` before the instruction tuning paragraph (L15) to try to save space
     ```latex
     \looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically...
     ```
   
   - **`docs/paper/conclusion.tex`** — Current state after two rounds of compression:
     ```latex
     \vspace{-1mm}
     \section{Conclusion}
     \label{sec:conclusion}
     \vspace{-1mm}
     
     Recursive generation in base models produces a decomposable depth response---location drifts while shape is approximately preserved---enabling cross-generator depth classification (leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694; 3-class: 0.762) across five families, establishing depth as a complementary analytical dimension; extending DDR to instruction-tuned models remains open (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}).
     ```
     - Changes from v51: "five model families"→"five families", "analytical dimension complementary to model-collapse analyses"→"complementary analytical dimension", "instruction-tuned and frontier models"→"instruction-tuned models", "an open challenge"→"open", added `\vspace{-1mm}` before/after section header
   
   - **`docs/paper/appendix_confusion_matrix.tex`** — Split Figure 5 vertically using trim/clip

        "content": "\\begingroup\n\\setlength{\\parskip}{2pt plus 1pt}\n\n\\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.\nThe consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.\nShape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.\n$R^2$ values are computed from ordinary least-squares regression on depth-level means (five points per generator, one per depth 0--4), not per-sample regression; the annotated Spearman $\\rho$ provides the complementary per-sample monotonicity measure.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Bold line: pooled mean ($\\pm$1.96\\,SE shading) across all generators, colored by DDR zone (blue: drifting, wine: intermediate, amber: near-stationary). Thin traces: individual generators. Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\endgroup\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 356pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=356pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Colored by 3-class label}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) A gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}\n\\label{fig:umap}\n\\end{figure}\n",

        "totalLines": 35

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_3/projects/gen-depth-fingerprint/docs/paper/appendix_instruct.tex",
        "content": "\\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}\n\\label{tab:instruct}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{Binary F1}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{$\\boldsymbol{\\rho}$\\textbf{(ent.\\ mean)}} & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-7}\n\\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \\textbf{Rev.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\\\\nLlama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nInstruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\\Delta| \\leq 3.2$\\,pp).\nThe asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.\nEntropy-mean $\\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\\ 3 of 7 for Llama.\nThis pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Retraining on instruction-tuned data.}\nTo test whether the depth signal persists under instruction tuning with reversed polarity, we retrain the XGBoost classifier on instruction-tuned feature data (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-retrain}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Classification recovery under instruction-tuned retraining (binary-only: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, gen-0 excluded from training and evaluation). Base$\\rightarrow$Inst: base-trained classifier tested on instruct data. Note: Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reports higher instruct binary F1 (e.g., OLMo 0.813) because it uses 3-class training with binary evaluation, where gen-0 in training helps the classifier learn the feature space.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-retrain}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Base$\\rightarrow$Inst} & \\textbf{Retrained} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.321 & 0.590 & $+$0.269 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.500 & 0.660 & $+$0.160 \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B-Inst  & 0.532 & 0.600 & $+$0.068 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nRetraining recovers substantial within-model performance ($+$6.8--26.9\\,pp), but the retrained F1 (0.590--0.660) remains below base-model levels (0.69--0.72).\nThree-generator instruct LOGO yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.487, indicating weak cross-generator transfer under instruction tuning.\n\n\\paragraph{DDR structure under instruction tuning.}\n$k{=}2$ clustering in $(|\\rho|, d)$ space on pooled instruct features yields silhouette\\,=\\,0.372 (vs.\\ 0.660 for base models), indicating that the clean drifting/non-drifting partition is substantially compressed.\nThe compression is model-dependent: OLMo-Instruct shows near-zero $|\\rho|$ for TTR and hapax (0.006, 0.025), while Qwen instruct models retain moderate correlations ($|\\rho|{=}0.48$--$0.53$).\nCross-condition transfer is near chance in both directions (base$\\rightarrow$instruct binary F1\\,=\\,0.490; instruct$\\rightarrow$base F1\\,=\\,0.454), with the asymmetry ($-$0.036) suggesting that instruct-trained features generalize even less to base distributions than the reverse.\nThese results indicate that instruction tuning compresses the feature-space structure underlying DDR rather than simply reversing feature directions (Figure~\\ref{fig:instruct-ddr}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 205pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Base models (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=205pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_instruct_ddr.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Instruction-tuned models (silhouette\\,=\\,0.372)}\n\\caption{DDR partition under base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models. Bars show pooled $|d|$; markers show individual generators. Instruction tuning compresses all features toward zero, collapsing the partition. Entropy max shifts from near-stationary to intermediate; formerly drifting features (hapax, TTR) lose most of their effect size.}\n\\label{fig:instruct-ddr}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Reference model sensitivity.}\nOne hypothesis for the instruct degradation is reference-model mismatch: the base Pythia-410M reference may produce systematically biased NLL estimates on instruction-tuned outputs, masking depth-dependent variation.\nTo test this, we replace the reference with Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct and recompute all features on the two instruct generators.\nThe hypothesis is partially rejected: Qwen3-1.7B-Instruct shows mixed changes (entropy max $d$: $0.51 \\to 0.08$, becoming near-stationary; entropy std $d$: $1.07 \\to 1.51$, increasing), while OLMo-Instruct loses nearly all depth signal (entropy mean $d$: $1.33 \\to 0.02$; tail decay $d$: $0.60 \\to 0.06$).\nThe model-dependent heterogeneity rules out reference mismatch as a universal explanation for instruct DDR degradation, supporting the interpretation that alignment fundamentally reshapes the generation dynamics rather than introducing a correctable measurement artifact.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end instruct classification.}\nTo test whether depth signal persists at the token level despite summary-statistic degradation, we apply the QLoRA end-to-end classifier (Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) to instruction-tuned data using within-model 5-fold stratified CV (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{End-to-end QLoRA vs.\\ XGBoost on instruction-tuned data (within-model 5-fold CV, Qwen3-0.6B backbone). Base QLoRA rows shown for reference only; instruct sets ($N{\\approx}4.4$--$5.0$K) are approximately twice the base sets ($N{\\approx}2.3$K), so cross-condition comparison is confounded by training set size.}\n\\label{tab:instruct-qlora}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{3-class F1}} & & \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3}\n\\textbf{Model} & XGB & QLoRA & $\\boldsymbol{\\Delta}$ & \\textbf{$N$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B-Inst   & 0.575 & 0.888\\,$\\pm$\\,0.007 & $+$31.3\\,pp & 4{,}429 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B-Inst  & 0.732 & 0.907\\,$\\pm$\\,0.008 & $+$17.5\\,pp & 4{,}963 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B (base) &       & 0.859\\,$\\pm$\\,0.004 &             & 2{,}379 \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B (base)&       & 0.904\\,$\\pm$\\,0.009 &             & 2{,}291 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe XGBoost-to-QLoRA gap on the same instruct data is substantial ($+$17.5 to $+$31.3\\,pp), indicating that depth signal persists at the token level under instruction tuning but is inaccessible to the seven summary statistics; the degradation reported in Table~\\ref{tab:instruct} reflects a feature-level limitation rather than signal absence.\nNote that instruct samples are approximately twice the base count used here (Table~\\ref{tab:instruct-qlora}), so the instruct-vs-base QLoRA comparison is confounded by training set size; the meaningful comparison is QLoRA-vs-XGBoost on the same instruct data.\nAll results use within-model 5-fold CV, not cross-generator LOGO, limiting generalization claims.\n",
        "numLines": 101,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 101
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/appendix_instruct.tex"
  },

        "content": "To verify that the depth signal resides in the features rather than the classifier,\nwe repeat the 6-generator LOGO evaluation using Logistic Regression and Random Forest\nin place of XGBoost (Table~\\ref{tab:alt-classifier}).\nAll other settings (the same 7 features, 3-class labels, and class-balanced downsampling) are\nkept identical. For Logistic Regression we apply standard scaling to the features.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{LOGO macro-F1 across three classifiers. Each column shows the held-out generator F1. (6-generator LOGO; 3-class.)}\n\\label{tab:alt-classifier}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lc cccccc}\n\\toprule\nClassifier & Avg F1 & OLMo-2 & Py-1B & Py-2.8B & Qw-0.6B & Qw-1.7B & Qw-8B \\\\\n\\midrule\nXGBoost          & 0.789 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.824 & 0.806 & 0.683 \\\\\nRandom Forest    & 0.790 & 0.764 & 0.856 & 0.804 & 0.825 & 0.807 & 0.684 \\\\\nLogistic Regr.   & 0.725 & 0.719 & 0.781 & 0.740 & 0.745 & 0.743 & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nBoth tree-based classifiers achieve LOGO F1 $\\approx 0.79$,\nand even a linear model reaches $0.725$, well above the $0.33$ chance baseline for\nthree classes.\nThe per-generator ranking is also consistent across classifiers\n(Pythia-1B easiest, Qwen3-8B hardest),\nconfirming that discriminative power originates in the entropy and lexical features,\nnot in classifier capacity.\nOrdinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) achieve comparable F1 with overlapping CIs, which confirms that 3-class granularity provides insufficient resolution for ranking-aware losses to improve over standard multiclass classification.\n\n\\subsection*{Sampling Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{app:sampling-sensitivity}\n\nTo assess robustness to decoding temperature, we regenerate continuation chains at $t \\in \\{0.5, 0.9\\}$ with fixed nucleus $p{=}0.9$ (matching the default) for four generators spanning three model families: Pythia-2.8B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, and Qwen3-0.6B (Table~\\ref{tab:sampling-ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Depth classification under varying temperature (fixed top-$p$\\,=\\,0.9). Binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+. LOGO evaluation; 95\\% bootstrap CIs.$^{\\ast}$$^{\\dagger}$}\n\\label{tab:sampling-ablation}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc c}\n\\toprule\nModel & Temp & LOGO Binary F1 & 95\\% CI & $\\rho$(ent.\\ mean) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.5  & \\textbf{0.814} & [.798, .828] & $-0.58$ \\\\\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.740 & [.725, .755] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nPythia-2.8B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.480 & [.465, .498] & $-0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.5  & \\textbf{0.783} & [.768, .798] & $-0.72$ \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.7  & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nOLMo-2-1B & 0.9  & 0.453 & [.437, .469] & $-0.26$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.5  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.7  & 0.751 & --- & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-1.7B$^{\\dagger}$ & 0.9  & 0.566 & --- & $-0.37$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.5  & 0.711 & [.694, .729] & $-0.67$ \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.7  & 0.691 & [.673, .709] & $-0.60$ \\\\\nQwen3-0.6B & 0.9  & 0.598 & [.582, .615] & $-0.29$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\begin{minipage}{\\columnwidth}\n\\scriptsize $^{\\ast}$OLMo temperature experiments use OLMo-2-0425-1B (April 2025 release); the original OLMo-2-1B-hf weights are no longer publicly available.\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n$^{\\dagger}$Pythia-2.8B and Qwen3-1.7B use 6-generator LOGO (pre-expansion); OLMo-2-1B and Qwen3-0.6B use 8-generator LOGO. Within-model comparisons are unaffected.\n\\end{minipage}\n\n\\begin{sloppypar}\nTemperature sensitivity is asymmetric and model-dependent. High-temperature degradation ($t{=}0.9$) is universal: all four generators lose $9.3$--$26.0$\\,pp in binary F1, with entropy mean's $|\\rho|$ weakening to $0.26$--$0.37$ (vs.\\ $0.58$--$0.72$ at $t{=}0.5$). Low-temperature improvement ($t{=}0.5$) is model-dependent: Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B gain $+7.4$ and $+8.8$\\,pp respectively, while both Qwen models show negligible change ($0$--$2.0$\\,pp). This split tracks model family rather than scale: Qwen3-0.6B (smallest) and Qwen3-1.7B both plateau, while OLMo-2-1B (similar scale to Qwen3-1.7B) benefits substantially. One possible explanation is that Qwen models produce more peaked output distributions at $t{=}0.7$, leaving less room for low-temperature compression to sharpen the depth signal.\n\\end{sloppypar}\n\nThe method operates reliably within $t \\in [0.5, 0.7]$ across all tested models. Degradation onset at $t{=}0.9$ defines the practical upper bound, consistent with the hypothesis that elevated temperature injects sufficient randomness to flatten the distributional drift that DDR exploits (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\subsection*{Per-Generator Performance}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:per-gen} visualizes per-generator LOGO performance.\nPythia models (1B, 2.8B) achieve highest F1, while larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) show degradation.\nThe scale effect (larger models $\\rightarrow$ lower F1) is consistent with higher-capacity models generating text statistically closer to human writing.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 347pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) 8-generator binary and 3-class F1, sorted by binary F1}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=347pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_per_gen.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) 6-generator LOGO detail heatmap}\n\\caption{Per-generator LOGO performance. Dashed lines in (a): pool average. Heatmap in (b) shows the performance spread across model families.}\n\\label{fig:per-gen}\n\\end{figure}\n",
        "numLines": 93,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 93
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/appendix_classifier.tex"
  },

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	%% Mechanism — why DDR exists
5	We hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of nucleus sampling: top-$p$ truncation preserves the conditional distribution's functional form while recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical, compressing expected NLL monotonically (Appendix~\ref{app:nll-distribution}); this hypothesis is testable but unproven.
6	Two testable predictions---increasing randomness weakens location drift, semantic constraints attenuate it---are both supported ($\tau{=}1.0$: $\rho{=}{-}0.002$; elaboration: F1\,=\,0.730; Appendices~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity},\ref{app:prompt-type}), while shape stability holds across decoding strategies ($\tau{=}1.0$, greedy, top-$k$; Appendices~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability},\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
7	
8	%% Implications — what DDR enables
9	Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for base-model corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access.
10	The classifier supports batch contamination estimation (${\pm}3$--$4$\%; Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}); depth-stratified curation has been proposed as a prophylactic against model collapse \cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though no downstream improvement was observed (Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}).
11	
12	%% Scope \& Connections — where DDR works and fails
13	DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-supplement}); DDR complements \citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).
14	These observations collectively suggest that recursive autoregressive generation produces rapidly diminishing inter-iteration differences, consistent with the practical resolution limit we observe.
15	\looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically: location features lose monotonic depth correlation (6/7 show $\rho$ reversal) while shape stationarity is partly preserved; the XGBoost classifier degrades by 3.7--19.9\,pp, but QLoRA recovers to F1\,=\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), suggesting depth signal persists in higher-dimensional representations.
16	Three depth classes represent the current practical resolution limit under available approaches, both handcrafted and end-to-end (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}); cross-domain signal persists (F1\,=\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\,pp at 5\%, F1\,=\,0.614 at 10\%; Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
17	
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All eight generators are base (pretrained) models with $\leq$8.2B parameters.
5	Instruction-tuned variants show substantially degraded depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), and Gemma-2-2B exhibits near-zero gen-1 F1 (binary F1\,=\,0.440; gen-1 nearly undetectable) due to tokenizer and attention differences (Appendix~\ref{app:9gen-scale}), indicating that architecture-specific generalization failures can occur; frontier models (70B+) remain untested.
6	
7	The evaluation assumes a fixed decoding regime (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$) with English Wikipedia seeds and an English-centric reference model (Pythia-410M); cross-temperature transfer degrades at mismatched settings (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-temp}), and non-English applicability is untested.
8	
9	Depth resolution is bounded at three classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+); finer-grained 5-class classification yields F1\,$\approx$\,0.50 with deeper depths mutually indistinguishable (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}).
10	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\nWe hypothesize that DDR reflects a structural property of nucleus sampling: top-$p$ truncation preserves the conditional distribution's functional form while recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical, compressing expected NLL monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}); this hypothesis is testable but unproven.\nTwo testable predictions---increasing randomness weakens location drift, semantic constraints attenuate it---are both supported ($\\tau{=}1.0$: $\\rho{=}{-}0.002$; elaboration: F1\\,=\\,0.730; Appendices~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity},\\ref{app:prompt-type}), while shape stability holds across decoding strategies ($\\tau{=}1.0$, greedy, top-$k$; Appendices~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability},\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).\n\n%% Implications — what DDR enables\nDepth constitutes a new analytical dimension for base-model corpus auditing that binary human-vs-machine classifiers cannot access.\nThe classifier supports batch contamination estimation (${\\pm}3$--$4$\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}); depth-stratified curation has been proposed as a prophylactic against model collapse \\cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though no downstream improvement was observed (Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}).\n\n%% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails\nDDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-supplement}); DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\nThese observations collectively suggest that recursive autoregressive generation produces rapidly diminishing inter-iteration differences, consistent with the practical resolution limit we observe.\n\\looseness=-1 Instruction tuning compresses DDR asymmetrically: location features lose monotonic depth correlation (6/7 show $\\rho$ reversal) while shape stationarity is partly preserved; the XGBoost classifier degrades by 3.7--19.9\\,pp, but QLoRA recovers to F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), suggesting depth signal persists in higher-dimensional representations.\nThree depth classes represent the current practical resolution limit under available approaches, both handcrafted and end-to-end (Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}); cross-domain signal persists (F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%, F1\\,=\\,0.614 at 10\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\n",
